[PATCH v2 2/2] KVM: selftests: Test steal-time dirty logging and RISC-V STA transitions
Author: Bingyu Xian <shanbeeyoo@gmail.com>

KVM writes to a guest steal-time structure must be recorded in the guest
RAM dirty log. Enable dirty logging on the shared-area memslot in the
common per-vCPU test path, clear initialization dirty bits after the
first run, and force runnable scheduling delay before checking that the
subsequent accounting write marks the expected page dirty.

On RISC-V, additionally exercise three SBI STA transitions: disabling an
active shared area, re-enabling the same area, and replacing an active
registration directly with a new area at the end of a second host page.
Clear the dirty log after each transition, arrange runnable contention on
the pinned CPU with a bounded monotonic timeout, and compare sequence and
stolen time against a stopped-vCPU snapshot so valid zero-increment skips
are permitted. After active replacement, verify that the old page remains
both unchanged and clean.

Use per-architecture hooks for protocol-specific lifecycle cases instead of
adding #ifdefs in main(). These SBI enable/disable operations match those
used by the Linux RISC-V guest CPU online and CPU-down callbacks, while the
selftest exercises the SBI protocol directly rather than booting Linux.
Drop the v1 MADV_DONTNEED check, which discarded guest page contents and
did not model migration.

Co-developed-by: Quan Zhou <zhouquan@iscas.ac.cn>
Signed-off-by: Quan Zhou <zhouquan@iscas.ac.cn>
Signed-off-by: Bingyu Xian <shanbeeyoo@gmail.com>
---
Changes in v2:
- Move accounting dirty-log checks into the common per-vCPU loop and
  remove the added #ifdefs from main().
- Test RISC-V disable, re-enable of the same area, and direct active
  replacement separately; drop the v1 MADV_DONTNEED case.
- Isolate registration dirty bits with kvm_vm_get_dirty_log() and arrange
  bounded runnable contention instead of assuming unconditional updates.
- Align the two-page region and boundary offset to getpagesize().

[PATCH v2 1/2] KVM: selftests: Fail explicitly when schedstat cannot be read
Author: Bingyu Xian <shanbeeyoo@gmail.com>

get_run_delay() currently passes a failed fopen() result directly to
fscanf(), and substitutes MIN_RUN_DELAY_NS when parsing fails. Steal-time
checks need real scheduling delay measurements, so neither dereferencing a
null stream nor returning a fabricated constant is suitable; repeated parse
failures also cause polling loops to spin indefinitely.

Assert that opening /proc/<tid>/schedstat and parsing both fields succeed.

Co-developed-by: Quan Zhou <zhouquan@iscas.ac.cn>
Signed-off-by: Quan Zhou <zhouquan@iscas.ac.cn>
Signed-off-by: Bingyu Xian <shanbeeyoo@gmail.com>
---

[PATCH v2 0/2] KVM: selftests: Test steal-time dirty logging and RISC-V STA transitions
Author: Bingyu Xian <shanbeeyoo@gmail.com>

This series extends the KVM steal_time selftest to cover guest RAM
dirty-log tracking for steal-time accounting writes across architectures,
and exercises RISC-V SBI STA shared-memory state transitions.

Patch 1 fixes get_run_delay() so that missing or malformed schedstat files
fail explicitly instead of dereferencing a null stream or returning a
fabricated constant that causes polling loops to spin indefinitely.

Patch 2 enables dirty logging on the shared-area memslot in the common
per-vCPU test path, clears initialization dirty bits before checking
accounting writes, removes the v1 #ifdefs from main(), and tests three
RISC-V SBI STA transitions: disabling an active area, re-enabling the same
area (matching the SBI calls used by Linux RISC-V guest CPU hotplug
callbacks), and replacing an active registration directly with a new area
at the end of a second host page.

Changes in v2:
- Split the get_run_delay() error-handling fix into a prerequisite patch.
- Move the steal-time accounting dirty-log check into the common per-vCPU
  test path and drop the added #ifdefs from main(). (Andrew Jones)
- Separate RISC-V SBI STA disable, re-enable of the same area, and direct
  replacement of an active registration; clarify that the selftest invokes
  SBI directly rather than running Linux CPU hotplug. (Andrew Jones)
- Drop the MADV_DONTNEED check, which discarded guest page contents rather
  than modeling migration. (Andrew Jones)
- Clear initialization and transition dirty bits before each accounting
  check so registration writes cannot mask missing accounting dirty marks.
- Force bounded runnable CPU contention instead of assuming every KVM_RUN
  advances the sequence counter when run_delay is unchanged.
- Size the RISC-V two-page region using the host page size for 16K/64K
  host page compatibility, and fix Author/Signed-off-by formatting.

v1: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261115085457.889873-1-shanbeeyoo@gmail.com/

Validation:
- RISC-V (QEMU TCG + H-ext L1 KVM): all 11 steal_time cases pass; local
  controls confirm compatibility with skipping zero-increment STA updates
  and immediate detection when the accounting dirty mark is omitted.
- Separate local 2-vCPU RISC-V Linux L2 guest run under L1 KVM confirms
  SBI STA detection and 3 CPU offline/online rounds invoking
  pv_time_cpu_down_prepare() and pv_time_cpu_online().
- x86_64 (host KVM): all 8 steal_time cases pass, plus fault-injection
  checks for missing and malformed schedstat files.

Note: AI tooling assisted with review analysis, test rework, and local
verification; all changes were reviewed and validated by the authors.

Thanks,
Bingyu

Bingyu Xian (2):
  KVM: selftests: Fail explicitly when schedstat cannot be read
  KVM: selftests: Test steal-time dirty logging and RISC-V STA
    transitions

[PATCH] KVM: x86: Reject SMM on VMs with private memory
Author: Younho Choi <gdool88@gmail.com>

A VM created with private memory (KVM_X86_SW_PROTECTED_VM, SEV-SNP, TDX)
reports a single memslot address space, because kvm_arch_nr_memslot_as_ids()
returns 1 when kvm_arch_has_private_mem() is true.  Nothing, however, prevents
such a VM's vCPU from entering SMM, in which case kvm_arch_vcpu_memslots_id()
still selects address space 1.  The existing comment "SMM is currently
unsupported for guests with private memory" is not enforced anywhere.

Because address space 1 is initialised with generation 1 and is never swapped
for a private-memory VM, while address space 0's generation advances by
kvm_arch_nr_memslot_as_ids() (i.e. 1) per swap, after a single memslot
operation both address spaces have generation 1.  The per-vCPU last_used_slot
cache in kvm_vcpu_gfn_to_memslot() validates only on the generation number, so
a slot cached in address space 0 is reused for an in-SMM (address space 1)
lookup.  After that slot is deleted and freed, the stale per-vCPU cache is
dereferenced: a host-kernel use-after-free of struct kvm_memory_slot reachable
by an unprivileged process that can open /dev/kvm.  The same aliasing also
leaves SMM-root SPTEs that mmu_notifier invalidation never zaps (it walks only
address space 0), and can trigger a BUG() in the TDP MMU.

Enforce the documented assumption: do not support SMM on VMs with private
memory.  Stop advertising KVM_CAP_X86_SMM and emulating MSR_IA32_SMBASE for
such VMs (which also makes kvm_inject_smi() and guest-driven SMIs fail), and
reject enabling SMM via KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS.

Fixes: 89ea60c2c7b5 ("KVM: x86: Add support for "protected VMs" that can utilize private memory")
Signed-off-by: Younho Choi <gdool88@gmail.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/svm/svm.c | 8 ++++++++
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c | 8 ++++++++
 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c     | 9 +++++++++
 3 files changed, 25 insertions(+)

[PATCH] vfio/virtio: read migration buffers and f_pos under migf->lock
Author: Qican Ma <goonsomeway@gmail.com>

virtiovf_resume_write() reads migf->buf and migf->buf_header and checks
filp->f_pos against vhca_buf->start_pos before taking state_mutex and
migf->lock. Because virtiovf_pci_resume_device_data() opens the file via
stream_open(), FMODE_ATOMIC_POS is cleared and concurrent writes through
duplicated file descriptors are not serialized by f_pos_lock. A racing
writer in VIRTIOVF_LOAD_STATE_PREP_HEADER_DATA can free and reallocate
migf->buf_header under migf->lock while another writer still holds the
stale pointer captured before the lock.

Move the buffer pointer reads and start_pos check under migf->lock after
checking VIRTIOVF_MIGF_STATE_ERROR.

Found with an LLM-assisted code review tool and verified by inspection on
v7.4-rc4 (b6a9fa34ce4). Build-tested only due to lack of virtio VF
live-migration hardware.

Fixes: 0bbc82e4ec79 ("vfio/virtio: Add support for the basic live
migration functionality")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Qican Ma <goonsomeway@gmail.com>
---
 drivers/vfio/pci/virtio/migrate.c | 13 +++++++++----
 1 file changed, 9 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

[PATCH] vfio/mlx5: read migration buffers and f_pos under migf->lock
Author: Qican Ma <goonsomeway@gmail.com>

mlx5vf_resume_write() caches migf->buf[0] and migf->buf_header[0] and
validates filp->f_pos before acquiring state_mutex and migf->lock.
Because the migration file is opened with stream_open(), FMODE_ATOMIC_POS
is cleared and concurrent writes on duplicated descriptors are not
serialized by f_pos_lock. A racing writer inside migf->lock can free and
reallocate migf->buf[0] or migf->buf_header[0] in PREP_HEADER_DATA or
PREP_IMAGE, leaving the unlocked caller with a stale or freed pointer.

Move the f_pos overflow check and buffer pointer reads under migf->lock
after the MLX5_MIGF_STATE_ERROR check, matching the pattern used in
commit 4ec5e932e636 ("vfio/qat: fix f_pos race in qat_vf_resume_write()").
Set done = -EINVAL rather than ret = -EINVAL on invalid f_pos so the
migration file is not transitioned into MLX5_MIGF_STATE_ERROR, preserving
existing behavior.

Found with an LLM-assisted code review tool and verified by inspection on
v7.4-rc4 (b6a9fa34ce4). Build-tested only due to lack of mlx5 VF
live-migration hardware.

Fixes: 6fadb021266d ("vfio/mlx5: Implement vfio_pci driver for mlx5 devices")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Qican Ma <goonsomeway@gmail.com>
---
 drivers/vfio/pci/mlx5/main.c | 17 +++++++++++------
 1 file changed, 11 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)

[PATCH] vfio: selftests: Add NVMe driver
Author: Josh Hilke <jrhilke@google.com>

Add a VFIO selftest driver for NVMe controllers. It binds to any PCI
function with the NVMe PCI class code 01:08:02, so vfio_pci_driver_test
can run on the NVMe SSDs that are common in servers, and on the nvme
device that QEMU emulates.

NVMe has no memory-to-memory copy command, so the driver implements a
memcpy through the logical blocks at the start of the first active
namespace. memcpy_start() writes @src to the logical blocks from LBA 0,
waits for the write, and then queues @count reads of the same blocks into
@dst. The driver must wait for the write because the controller can run
the commands of a queue in any order. All @count reads are in flight when
memcpy_start() returns. The driver spreads the reads over up to 16 such
queues, because one queue holds only as many commands as the controller
allows, 2047 on the QEMU device. The memcpy commands go on I/O queues
that have interrupts disabled. send_msi() sends a flush on one more I/O
queue that has interrupts enabled.

The driver overwrites the first max_memcpy_size bytes of the namespace.
Do not use a device that holds data you want to keep.

On some controllers, a DMA error from the unmapped-IOVA tests leaves the
submission queue in an error state that only a Function Level Reset
clears. After a failed memcpy, the driver disables the controller, resets
the function with VFIO_DEVICE_RESET, and initializes the controller
again.

The driver needs a controller that supports the NVM command set, 4 KiB
memory pages, at least two I/O queue pairs, and MSI-X, along with
a namespace that has no metadata and a logical block size of at most 4 KiB.
Controllers that need one of the quirks in drivers/nvme/host/pci.c are
not supported.

The driver uses the definitions in include/linux/nvme.h through a
symbolic link, like the other drivers do with their kernel headers.

Users can verify the driver in QEMU with these commands, after they
build the kernel and the VFIO selftests:

truncate -s 64M /tmp/nvme.img
vng \
--run arch/x86/boot/bzImage \
--user root \
--disable-microvm \
--memory 4G \
--cpus 4 \
--qemu-opts="-M q35,accel=kvm,kernel-irqchip=split" \
--qemu-opts="-device intel-iommu,intremap=on,caching-mode=on" \
--qemu-opts="-drive file=/tmp/nvme.img,if=none,id=nvm,format=raw" \
--qemu-opts="-device nvme,serial=deadbeef,drive=nvm,addr=10.0" \
--append "console=ttyS0 intel_iommu=on" \
--exec "modprobe vfio-pci && \
  ./tools/testing/selftests/vfio/scripts/setup.sh 0000:00:10.0 && \
  ./tools/testing/selftests/vfio/scripts/run.sh \
  ./tools/testing/selftests/vfio/vfio_pci_driver_test"

Signed-off-by: Josh Hilke <jrhilke@google.com>
---
Testing:
- Built VFIO and KVM selftests on x86_64 (dynamic and static) and VFIO selftests on arm64 (static).
- QEMU nvme (x86_64): vfio_pci_driver_test 35/35 passed (default and nsid=2); msix_qsize=1 and max_ioqpairs=1 failed at their expected assertions.
- Samsung PM175x VF (x86_64): vfio_pci_device_test 5/5, vfio_pci_driver_test 35/35, and KVM irq_test passed.
- Samsung PM174X VF (arm64): vfio_pci_device_test 5/5 and vfio_pci_driver_test 35/35 passed.

[PATCH] x86/fred: Add this_cpu_fred_sp() and export it for KVM
Author: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>

FRED adds fields to the VMCS host-state area for the stack pointers of
stack levels 1 to 3. The CPU loads these pointers on every VM exit to
ensure a valid FRED event delivery context is established immediately.
These values are per CPU, so KVM has to refresh them each time a vCPU is
loaded onto a CPU.

The stack for each level is assigned implicitly, when the FRED RSP MSRs
are programmed during CPU initialization. KVM could read the MSRs back,
but that costs three RDMSRs on every vCPU load, while the kernel already
has the values at hand. Add a helper that returns the corresponding
stack pointer for each FRED RSP MSR. Use it to program the MSRs during
init, and export it for KVM.

Key the helper on the MSR rather than on a stack level, so that KVM
never needs to know which exception stack backs which level. Eventually,
the same interface can be extended for the FRED shadow stack MSRs as
well.

Originally-by: Xin Li <xin@zytor.com>
Signed-off-by: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>
---
I have been working on splitting the FRED KVM series to make it easier
to review and merge. I am posting this separately because the patch has
a minor dependency on the ESTACK_ prefix patch merged in tip today. The
main goal is to collect acks from the x86 maintainers.

The first exported user for the helper is going to be in the FRED KVM
series, so I assume this would likely be merged via the KVM tree. You
can consider merging it via the x86 tree if you are feeling generous :)

Note, based on a quick check on a CWF system, reading the three FRED RSP
MSRs takes about ~6x longer than reading the per-CPU cached values.

v1:
 - Key the helper on the FRED RSP MSR rather than on a stack level.
   https://lore.kernel.org/lkml/82dc24e3-f574-4696-99cb-87c0c04eab93@intel.com/
 - Warn and return 0 on an unexpected MSR rather than calling BUG().
 - Add a comment saying why stack level 2 is special.
 - Drop Binbin's Reviewed-by and Xin's author tag, since the interface
   has changed.

FRED KVM v10:
 - https://lore.kernel.org/lkml/20260911213659.2025974-7-sohil.mehta@intel.com/
---

[RFC PATCH 25/26] KVM: x86/pmu: WARN if a mediated PMI arrives outside of guest IRQ handling
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Guest PMIs are delivered to KVM's mediated PMI handler while KVM handles
IRQs that are pending after a VM-Exit, either because the PMI caused the
VM-Exit, or because it was raised right before it.  Guest counters don't
count while host code runs (GLOBAL_CTRL is cleared on VM-Exit on Intel,
and events are guest-only on AMD), so guest PMIs shouldn't arrive at any
other time.

Now that the guest context can stay loaded while host code runs with
IRQs enabled, a PMI arriving outside of guest IRQ handling would most
likely be raised by a host PMU that perf failed to account for as
conflicting with the mediated PMU, and misrouted to the guest.  WARN in
that case to catch such bugs.

In theory, the WARN could fire for a guest PMI that is delivered late,
e.g. after KVM is done handling IRQs following a VM-Exit.  Checking the
guest's GLOBAL_STATUS for an overflow to rule this out doesn't work as
the guest can clear it, making the WARN guest-triggerable.  Such late
guest PMIs haven't been observed when stress testing on 8 different
platforms (Skylake, Cascade, Icelake, Sapphirerapids, Emeraldrapids,
Graniterapids, Genoa, or Turin).

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/pmu.c | 8 ++++++++
 1 file changed, 8 insertions(+)

[RFC PATCH 24/26] KVM: VMX: WARN if the Intel PT PMI handler runs for a mediated PMU
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

vmx_handle_intel_pt_intr() handles ToPA PMIs for guests using Intel PT
by setting the ToPA bit in KVM's software copy of the guest's
GLOBAL_STATUS, and requesting a PMI for the guest.  This doesn't work
with a mediated PMU, as the guest's GLOBAL_STATUS lives in hardware
while the guest context is loaded, and the software copy is overwritten
when the guest context is put.  The GLOBAL_STATUS accessors can't be
used instead, as the handler runs in NMI context.

The handler can't run for a vCPU with a mediated PMU though: while the
guest context is loaded, the LVTPC routes PMIs to the mediated PMI
vector, and perf ignores NMIs (see perf_event_nmi_handler()).  NMIs from
the guest are handled right after the VM-Exit, before the guest context
can be put.  WARN if it runs anyway.

Don't bail after the WARN, as handling the PMI is still the best effort:
the guest still gets a PMI, and setting the ToPA bit in the software
copy is either correct (if the guest context is not loaded) or
harmlessly lost.  Bailing would drop the guest's PMI, and let perf
handle the interrupt as a host Intel PT interrupt.

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c | 10 ++++++++++
 1 file changed, 10 insertions(+)

[RFC PATCH 26/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Allow emulation in the fastpath with a mediated PMU
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Effectively revert commit f7a65e58d643 ("KVM: x86/pmu: Disallow
emulation in the fastpath if mediated PMCs are active"), as KVM no
longer relies on its software copies of PMU state being consistent with
hardware while the guest context is loaded.

KVM now accesses PMU registers in hardware if needed with the guest
context loaded, and can therefore emulate instructions correctly in the
fastpath with a mediated PMU. Specifically, KVM writes to hardware when
incrementing counters or setting bits in GLOBAL_STATUS if the guest
context is loaded.

When emulating an instruction in the fastpath, KVM increments PMCs that
count instructions with the guest context loaded.  KVM now updates
counters and GLOBAL_STATUS in hardware in that case.
kvm_pmu_trigger_event() reads GLOBAL_CTRL from software to determine
which counters to increment, but the software copy is up to date even
when the guest context is loaded: either guest writes are intercepted,
or it's synced from hardware after every VM-Exit

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---

[RFC PATCH 23/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Add stats for mediated PMU context switches
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Add per-vCPU stats counting how many times the guest's mediated PMU
context is put, and why, mainly to help debug performance problems
caused by frequent guest context switches, e.g. due to host perf events
conflicting with the mediated PMU.  The stats are also useful for
testing. Add:

- pmu_ctx_switches: all puts of the guest context.

- pmu_ctx_switches_vmexit: puts on VM-Exits, because perf has events
  conflicting with the mediated PMU.  Note, the guest context is only
  put on slowpath VM-Exits, not on every VM-Exit.

- pmu_ctx_switches_reclaim: puts because perf reclaimed the mediated
  PMU.

Don't add a stat for puts when the vCPU is put, e.g. when the vCPU task
is scheduled out or KVM_RUN returns to userspace, as it can be derived
from the other stats, and when exactly the vCPU is put is an
implementation detail.

Return whether the guest context was actually put from
kvm_mediated_pmu_put(), as it may already be put when perf reclaims the
mediated PMU.  The guest context is always loaded on slowpath VM-Exits
for vCPUs with a mediated PMU, so WARN if it wasn't put there, and count
the put unconditionally.

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---

[RFC PATCH 22/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Put the mediated PMU context lazily on slowpath VM-Exits
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Instead of putting the guest's mediated PMU context on every slowpath
VM-Exit, only put it on slowpath VM-Exits if perf has events conflicting
with the mediated PMU, i.e. events on mediated-capable PMUs or on PMUs
that deliver interrupts like guest PMIs.  Otherwise, leave the guest
context loaded while handling slowpath VM-Exits, and put it when the
vCPU is put (including when the vCPU task is scheduled out), or when
perf reclaims the mediated PMU before accessing it for the host.

Saving and restoring the guest's PMU state on every slowpath VM-Exit is
expensive.  Below is the throughput (median of 10 x 5s runs) of a CPUID
loop in a guest and of netperf TCP_RR between two guests, on AMD Turin
and Intel Granite Rapids, with a mediated PMU before (eager) and after
(lazy) this change, and without a vPMU.  The rows are without perf
events (idle), and with perf events in the guest, in the host, or both:

                          Turin                   Granite Rapids
                 eager     lazy   no vPMU     eager     lazy   no vPMU
  CPUID/s:
    idle        547.8K  1688.6K  1816.5K     687.9K  1386.2K  1352.5K
    guest perf  483.2K  1484.8K        -     631.6K  1266.7K        -
    host perf   342.1K   352.5K        -     418.4K   411.4K        -
    both        339.9K   338.4K        -     403.4K   396.2K        -
  TCP_RR/s:
    idle         43.4K    49.9K    50.4K      12.1K    13.7K    13.8K
    guest perf   42.5K    50.0K        -      12.0K    13.7K        -
    host perf    39.5K    40.1K        -      11.8K    12.0K        -
    both         39.7K    40.0K        -      11.8K    12.0K        -

Without perf events, CPUID throughput improves by 208% on Turin and 102%
on Granite Rapids, reaching 93% and 100% of the throughput without a
vPMU, and TCP_RR throughput improves by 15% and 13%, reaching 99% of the
throughput without a vPMU.  With host perf events, KVM still puts the
guest context on every slowpath VM-Exit, and throughput is within 3% of
before.

KVM already accesses guest PMU state in hardware while the guest context
is loaded, including the LVTPC.  Conflicting events can only be added
after perf reclaims the mediated PMU, and conflicts are checked with
IRQs disabled after the VM-Exit, so host events never run with the guest
context loaded.  As loading and putting the guest context is idempotent,
it doesn't matter which path puts it first.

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

[RFC PATCH 18/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Access GLOBAL_STATUS in hardware if the guest context is loaded
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

In preparation for leaving the guest's mediated PMU context loaded
during slowpath VM-Exit handling, read and write the guest's
GLOBAL_STATUS in hardware if the guest context is loaded.  Hardware sets
overflow bits in GLOBAL_STATUS while the guest runs, and KVM's software
copy is only synced from hardware when the guest context is put.

Add helpers to read GLOBAL_STATUS, refreshing the software copy, and to
clear and set bits via GLOBAL_STATUS_CLR and GLOBAL_STATUS_SET
(GLOBAL_OVF_CTRL and GLOBAL_STATUS_SET on Intel), updating both the
software copy and hardware. Use the helpers when signaling overflow of
counters incremented for emulated instructions too, and disable IRQs
across the whole increment to avoid toggling IRQs in each helper. Add
the MSR indices to kvm_pmu_ops for use in common code.

As with counters, disable IRQs so that checking whether the guest
context is loaded and accessing hardware are atomic with respect to perf
reclaiming the mediated PMU (via IPI) and putting the guest context.

Document GLOBAL_STATUS as state that's read from hardware while the
guest context is loaded.

[RFC PATCH 21/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Make loading/putting the mediated PMU context idempotent
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Make kvm_mediated_pmu_load() and kvm_mediated_pmu_put() bail if the
guest context is already loaded or put, respectively, in preparation for
leaving the guest context loaded across slowpath VM-Exits.
kvm_mediated_pmu_load() will still be called on every vcpu_enter_guest(),
even if the guest context is still loaded, and kvm_mediated_pmu_put()
will be called from multiple paths, e.g. on slowpath VM-Exits, on
vcpu_put(), and when perf reclaims the mediated PMU, any of which may
find the guest context already put.

Checking the per-CPU state is sufficient, as only the running vCPU's
guest context can be loaded on a CPU: it is put on vcpu_put() before
another vCPU can run.

Move lockdep_assert_irqs_disabled() before the checks, so that callers
are verified to have IRQs disabled even if loading/putting is skipped.

[RFC PATCH 16/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Write GLOBAL_CTRL to hardware if the guest context is loaded
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

In preparation for leaving the guest's mediated PMU context loaded
during slowpath VM-Exit handling, write the guest's GLOBAL_CTRL to
hardware on AMD if the guest context is loaded.  On Intel, the guest's
GLOBAL_CTRL lives in the VMCS and is loaded by hardware on VM-Enter, so
intel_pmu_write_global_ctrl() already writes it to the right place.

Add kvm_pmu_set_global_ctrl() to update the software copy and forward
the value to vendor code, and implement write_global_ctrl() for AMD
using kvm_pmu_guest_context_wrmsr().  Disable IRQs across the update, so
that checking whether the guest context is loaded and writing the MSR
are atomic with respect to perf reclaiming the mediated PMU (via IPI)
and putting the guest context.

Disabling IRQs isn't needed on Intel, as the VMCS isn't affected by perf
reclaiming the PMU, but special casing Intel isn't worth the trouble.
Alternatively, the software update and disabling IRQs could be moved
into vendor code, but the software copy also needs to be updated for
the emulated PMU (which doesn't use write_global_ctrl()), so keeping
the update in common code is simpler.

Document GLOBAL_CTRL as a control that's read from the software copy and
written through to hardware while the guest context is loaded.

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

[RFC PATCH 19/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Sync the guest's LVTPC to hardware if the guest context is loaded
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

In preparation for leaving the guest's mediated PMU context loaded
during slowpath VM-Exit handling, propagate writes to the guest's
virtual LVTPC to hardware if the guest context is loaded.

The guest's LVTPC is emulated by KVM: guest writes are intercepted and
only update the virtual LVTPC, and its mask bit is only written to the
hardware LVTPC when the guest context is loaded.  Currently, the guest
context is put on every VM-Exit that isn't handled in the fastpath and
loaded again before the next VM-Enter, and fastpath handlers never write
the virtual LVTPC, so changes reach hardware before the guest runs
again.  Once the guest context stays loaded across slowpath VM-Exits,
changes wouldn't reach hardware until the guest context is put and
loaded again.

For example, KVM masks the virtual LVTPC when delivering a PMI.  If the
guest context is loaded while the virtual LVTPC is masked and then stays
loaded, the guest unmasking LVTPC at the end of its PMI handler would
only update the virtual LVTPC, leaving the hardware LVTPC masked, and
the guest would stop getting PMIs.

Hook kvm_lapic_set_reg(), which handles all writes to the virtual
LVTPC, except for kvm_apic_set_state() restoring the APIC state from
userspace, which can't happen with the guest context loaded.

RFC note: Intel CPUs also set the mask bit in the hardware LVTPC when
delivering a PMI.  KVM emulates this by masking the virtual LVTPC, which
is now propagated to hardware, but it doesn't do so if the guest's CPUID
isn't Intel-compatible, or if delivering the PMI fails (e.g. the guest's
APIC is hardware-disabled, or the virtual LVTPC has an invalid delivery
mode).  In those cases, the hardware LVTPC stays masked while the guest
context stays loaded, instead of being unmasked at the next VM-Enter.
Cross-vendor configurations aren't supported with a mediated PMU, and
the other cases require a misconfigured guest, so this is ignored for
now.  If it matters, kvm_pmu_deliver_pmi() could resync the hardware
LVTPC when the virtual LVTPC remains unmasked.

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/lapic.c |  4 ++++
 arch/x86/kvm/pmu.c   | 15 +++++++++++++++
 arch/x86/kvm/pmu.h   |  1 +
 3 files changed, 20 insertions(+)

[RFC PATCH 15/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Write PMU controls to hardware on reprogram if loaded
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

In preparation for leaving the guest's mediated PMU context loaded
during slowpath VM-Exit handling, write eventsel_hw and
fixed_ctr_ctrl_hw to hardware when reprogramming counters if the guest
context is loaded.

Only update eventsel_hw and fixed_ctr_ctrl_hw when reprogramming
counters, instead of also updating them when the guest writes the MSRs,
to keep them in sync with hardware and keep the hardware writes in one
place. Otherwise, if e.g. eventsel_hw is zeroed on an MSR write and the
guest context is put before reprogramming, putting the context would
skip clearing the event selector in hardware.

As fixed_ctr_ctrl_hw is no longer overwritten with the guest's value on
MSR writes, compare against the guest's previous fixed_ctr_ctrl in
reprogram_fixed_counters() again, effectively reverting commit
9c6e2463c9f3 ("KVM: x86/pmu: Use hardware value when reprogramming for
FIXED_CTR_CTRL changes").  Comparing against fixed_ctr_ctrl_hw was only
needed because the overwrite could leave counters that are disallowed by
the event filter enabled in fixed_ctr_ctrl_hw without reprogramming
them.

Disable IRQs across updating the software fields and writing hardware,
so that the two are atomic with respect to perf reclaiming the mediated
PMU (via IPI) and putting the guest context. Document which guest PMU
registers are read from software and written through to hardware while
the guest context is loaded.

[RFC PATCH 13/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Compute eventsel_hw from eventsel when reprogramming
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Instead of only setting the enable bit in eventsel_hw when refreshing
the event filter, compute the entire eventsel_hw from eventsel when
reprogramming a counter that's allowed to count, and rename
kvm_mediated_pmu_refresh_event_filter() to
kvm_mediated_pmu_reprogram_counter() accordingly. As counters are only
allowed to count if the enable bit is set in eventsel, copying eventsel
also sets the enable bit.

Move forcing GUESTONLY and clearing HOSTONLY on AMD from
amd_pmu_set_msr() to the reprogramming path, using vendor-provided bits
to set and clear, and have amd_pmu_set_msr() record the guest's value
as-is like Intel. Writing an event selector always requests a reprogram,
which is handled before the guest context is loaded, so the value is
fixed up before it's written to hardware.

For counters that can't count, the event selector in hardware may now
differ in the GUESTONLY and HOSTONLY bits, which is benign as the enable
bit is cleared.

[RFC PATCH 12/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Access PMCs in hardware if the mediated PMU context is loaded
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

In preparation for leaving the guest's mediated PMU context loaded
during slowpath VM-Exit handling, read and write the guest's counters in
hardware if the guest context is loaded.  While the guest context is
loaded, the guest's counter values live in hardware, and KVM's software
copies in pmc->counter may be stale, as they are only synced from
hardware when the context is put.

Read counters with RDPMC, refreshing the software copy, and write them
through to hardware in addition to updating the software copy.  Use the
accessors when incrementing counters for emulated instructions too,
instead of updating pmc->counter directly.  Updating the software copy
while the guest context is loaded isn't strictly necessary, as it's
synced from hardware when the context is put.  This could be optimized
by tracking whether the software copy is up-to-date.

guest context is loaded, as it will be reused for other guest PMU
registers, and document there which guest PMU state lives in hardware
while the guest context is loaded.

Disable IRQs across checking whether the guest context is loaded and
accessing the counter, as perf may reclaim the PMU via IPI and put the
guest context.

[RFC PATCH 10/26] perf: Track mediated PMU conflicts
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Track perf events conflicting with the mediated PMU, which are events on
mediated-capable PMUs, or on PMUs using the same PMI delivery mechanism
as the guest PMIs (LVTPC on x86). Add a new PMU capability for the
latter, and set it for Intel PT and Intel BTS.

Whether or not perf has events conflicting with the mediated PMU will be
used by KVM to determine if putting the guest PMU context on the VM-exit
slowpath is necessary.

Intel BTS is never active while the guest runs, as KVM doesn't allow the
guest to set DEBUGCTL.TR/BTS and VM-exit clears DEBUGCTL. Today, its
interrupts can only be delivered to the guest in the short windows
between loading the guest context and VM-entry, and between VM-exit and
putting the guest context. Tracking it keeps those windows from
extending to slowpath VM-exit handling.

Intel PT (in system mode) keeps tracing while the guest runs, so its
interrupts can already be delivered to the guest today. Tracking it
keeps that from extending to slowpath VM-exit handling.

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

[RFC PATCH 09/26] perf/x86: Reclaim the mediated PMU before clearing dirty counters
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

perf_clear_dirty_counters() zeroes PMCs that aren't assigned to host
events before enabling RDPMC for a task, so that it can't read stale
values. It doesn't go through remote_function(), so it doesn't reclaim
the PMU, and if a guest's mediated PMU context is loaded, the "dirty"
counters hold guest state that clearing would clobber.

In preparation for KVM leaving the guest context loaded with IRQs
enabled, reclaim the PMU before clearing dirty counters. Do so before
filtering out the counters assigned to host events, as host events are
scheduled out while the guest context is loaded, and putting the guest
context schedules them back in, assigning counters to them.

No functional change intended, as the guest context is currently only
loaded with IRQs disabled, where perf_clear_dirty_counters() never runs

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

[RFC PATCH 05/26] perf/x86: Don't snapshot the branch stack while a guest PMU is loaded
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

The branch stack snapshot callbacks behind perf_snapshot_branch_stack()
(used by bpf_get_branch_snapshot()) freeze the LBRs by disabling the
PMU, copy the entries, and re-enable the PMU.  If a guest's mediated
vPMU context is loaded, e.g. if the snapshot is taken from NMI context
or within KVM's inner run loop, re-enabling the PMU writes the host's
GLOBAL_CTRL over the guest's PMU state.

On Intel, guest counters then count host code (and can raise PMIs
delivered to the guest) until the next VM-Enter.  On AMD, the guest's
GLOBAL_CTRL itself is overwritten.  KVM putting the guest context lazily
would make this worse, as the guest context would also stay loaded while
handling slowpath VM-Exits, e.g. across KVM tracepoints.

The snapshot isn't useful either: the host LBR users (core PMU events)
are scheduled out while a guest context is loaded, so the LBRs aren't
read, and callers get stale entries from the last time the LBRs were
read for the host.

Return an empty snapshot if a guest context is loaded, before touching
the PMU. Callers already handle empty snapshots, e.g.
bpf_get_branch_snapshot() returns -ENOENT for 0 entries, which the
default static call returns without LBR support.

Use unlikely() to hint to the compiler to lay out the not-taken path as
the fall-through, so that the common path doesn't add a taken branch to
the snapshot. Both GCC 15.2 and Clang 18.1.8 do this with the unlikely()
hint, and emit a taken branch on the common path without it (GCC's
-freorder-blocks, enabled at -O2 and -Os, uses __builtin_expect()
probabilities to reduce taken branches [1]). If the hint isn't honored,
the worst case is one extra branch entry in the snapshot.

[1] https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gcc/Optimize-Options.html#index-freorder-blocks

Fixes: 42457a7fb6cac ("perf: Add APIs to load/put guest mediated PMU context")
Cc: Andrii Nakryiko <andrii@kernel.org>
Cc: Song Liu <song@kernel.org>
Cc: bpf@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 arch/x86/events/amd/core.c   |  8 ++++++++
 arch/x86/events/intel/core.c | 16 ++++++++++++++++
 2 files changed, 24 insertions(+)

[RFC PATCH 04/26] perf/x86: Use irqsave guard in branch stack snapshotting callbacks

guard(irqsave)() instead of local_irq_{save,restore}(), which also drops
the local flags variable. IRQs are no longer restored in
__intel_pmu_snapshot_branch_stack(), but in its callers.

The guard saves IRQs at the same point local_irq_save() did and restores
them at scope exit, after the PMU and LBR are re-enabled, so the

[RFC PATCH 00/26] KVM: x86/pmu: Dynamic mediated PMU context switching
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

When KVM uses the mediated PMU, it loads the guest context and puts it around
the inner fastpath loop, causing an expensive switch on every slowpath VM-Exit.
On microbenchmarks like CPUID, this can result in up to 3x drop in throughput
even when the host is not using the core PMU. On more realistic benchmarks like
netperf TCP_RR, it results in up to ~15% regression (at least in some setups).

This series uses a dynamic scheme for PMU context switching instead. On
slowpath VM-Exit, KVM only puts the guest context if host perf has events
conflicting with the mediated PMU. Otherwise, the guest context remains loaded
until either the vCPU is put (e.g. on context switch or return to userspace),
or perf "reclaims" it. This recovers most/all performance (at least on CPUID
and netperf) with a mediated PMU in the absence of host events. See "KVM:
x86/pmu: Put the mediated PMU context lazily on slowpath VM-Exits" for exact
numbers.

The Perf Side
-------------

Perf reclaims the PMU before executing any remote call on a CPU if the guest
context is loaded, including installing events, enabling events, etc. This is
too conservative, as perf really only needs to reclaim the PMU before an event
is both installed and enabled, but it results in a simpler and more
future-proof implementation.

There are a couple of other hiccups that perf needs to deal with outside of
reclaiming on remote calls, namely:

1. Intel PT/BTS. These PMUs are not mediated, but they use the same PMI
delivery mechanism as the mediated core PMU (i.e. LVTPC). Since they are not
mediated, they are not scheduled out while the guest context is loaded, and
their PMIs can be delivered into the guest instead of the host. Today, BTS is
never active while the guest runs (KVM doesn't allow the guest to enable it,
and VM-Exit clears DEBUGCTL), so this is limited to short windows around
VM-Entry/VM-Exit. Intel PT (in system mode) keeps tracing while the guest runs,
so its PMIs raised while the guest is running are delivered to the guest.

Lazily putting the guest context would make this worse for both, as host code
with tracing enabled could run with the guest context loaded for much longer,
most notably for BTS, which would go from short windows around VM-Entry/VM-Exit
to all of slowpath VM-Exit handling. To avoid that, this series tracks PMUs
that share the same PMI mechanism, and having events on any of them prompts KVM
to give up the PMU on slowpath VM-Exits, keeping the status quo.

Long-term, perhaps it would be better to schedule out Intel PT events while
the guest context is loaded, or somehow demux the PMIs?

2. Handling bpf_get_branch_snapshot() while the guest context is loaded (a
pre-existing bug that is more exposed by this series).

3. Clearing dirty counters when events are mmap()'d or the rdpmc attribute is
updated, which needs to reclaim the PMU first if the guest context is loaded.

NOTE: The first 5 patches fix pre-existing problems (or prepare for those
fixes) that are relevant to the series (or the dynamic switch makes them worse),
but they can be merged independently.

The KVM Side
------------

The main change is needing to access guest PMU registers in hardware while the
guest context is loaded. More specifically, the currently virtualized registers
fall into one of two categories:

1. Registers that can be read from software but must be written to hardware
(i.e. are always up-to-date in software). This includes registers that are
always intercepted or sync'd on every VM-exit, namely control registers:
eventsel, fixed_ctr_ctrl, and GLOBAL_CTRL.

2. Registers that must be read and written from hardware (i.e. software can
always be stale). This includes registers that can be updated by the guest or
hardware while the guest runs, namely counters and GLOBAL_STATUS.

Additionally, KVM needs to keep the physical and virtual LVTPC in sync while
the guest context is loaded.

KVM accessing registers in hardware as needed allows it to handle fastpath
instruction emulation with a mediated PMU, so this restriction is dropped.

Testing
-------

This was tested by new (AI-generated) extensions to PMU selftests that mostly
act as regression tests for the existing PMU, a selftest-style stress test, and
stressing perf with a real Linux VM. The selftests will be cleaned up and sent
out separately.

The full KVM x86 selftests and kvm-unit-tests were also run on Intel and AMD,
with no new failures compared to the base kernel.

Testing was done with Zide's "KVM: SVM: Drop counter MSR checks in
amd_is_valid_msr()" [1] applied, as without it saving vCPU state on AMD fails
when the guest has fewer counters than the host (KVM_GET_MSRS fails on the
unexposed counter MSRs).

[PATCH v2 2/2] KVM: arm64: nv: Avoid duplicate guest stage-2 mapping entries
Author: Colton Lewis <coltonlewis@google.com>

kvm_record_guest_s2_mapping() unconditionally inserts a new entry into
both guest_s2_mappings interval trees. Parallel stage-2 faults on the
same IPA or retries after -EAGAIN can therefore insert identical
entries for the same nested and canonical IPA range.

Check mmu->guest_s2_mappings under guest_s2_tracking_lock before
inserting, and free the new entry if an identical one is already
present.

Signed-off-by: Colton Lewis <coltonlewis@google.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/nested.c | 29 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 29 insertions(+)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH v2 1/2] KVM: arm64: nv: Discard undersized TTL hints in compute_tlb_inval_range()
Author: Colton Lewis <coltonlewis@google.com>

When an L1 guest issues TLBI IPAS2E1 with a non-zero TTL hint,
compute_tlb_inval_range() takes it at face value. If the hinted size is
smaller than the actual mapping (for example, a 4kB L3 hint on a 2MB
block), kvm_pgtable_stage2_unmap() clears the entire block while
kvm_remove_guest_s2_mappings() leaves its stale tracking entry behind in
guest_s2_mappings. On 16kB or 64kB hosts, a 4kB hint also triggers
WARN_ON(size & ~PAGE_MASK) in kvm_stage2_unmap_range().

A TTL hint smaller than PAGE_SIZE or the mapped entry is wrong. Check
it against PAGE_SIZE, the shadow stage-2 leaf size, and the guest block
size encoded in KVM_NV_GUEST_MAP_SZ. If it is too small, ignore the hint
and fall through to the unhinted invalidation path.

When falling back to the maximum mapping extent with stage-2 disabled,
size it from PAGE_SIZE rather than VTCR_EL2.TG0 so 1:1 shadow blocks are
still covered.

Fixes: d1de1576dc21 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Handle FEAT_TTL hinted TLB operations")
Signed-off-by: Colton Lewis <coltonlewis@google.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/nested.c | 76 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++------------
 1 file changed, 55 insertions(+), 21 deletions(-)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH v2 0/2] KVM: arm64: nv: Fix stale and duplicate guest S2
Author: Colton Lewis <coltonlewis@google.com>

When an L1 guest issues TLBI IPAS2E1 with a TTL hint smaller than the
actual mapping, KVM clears the entire block from the shadow stage-2
page table while leaving the stale tracking entry in guest_s2_mappings.
Patch 1 drops undersized TTL hints in compute_tlb_inval_range() and
falls through to the unhinted path so the unmap and tracking removal
cover the same range. This follows Marc's suggestion [2] on Dan
Carpenter's sub-page TTL patch [1] (which Patch 1 overlaps with and
subsumes), fixing WARN_ON(size & ~PAGE_MASK) on 16kB/64kB hosts as well.

Patch 2 avoids inserting identical duplicate entries in
kvm_record_guest_s2_mapping().

Tool-generated content:
- Initial bug discovery: automated LLM-assisted audit of the arm64
  nested stage-2 tracking code.
- Patch and text drafting: interactive Gemini session (patches, commit
  messages, and cover letter).

Changes since v1:
- Drop the kvm_remove_guest_s2_mappings() change; discard undersized TTL
  hints in compute_tlb_inval_range() and fall through to the unhinted
  path (Marc Zyngier).
- Split duplicate suppression in kvm_record_guest_s2_mapping() into a
  separate patch, call kfree() under guard(spinlock), and drop the Fixes
  tag (Wei-Lin Chang).
- Rewrite the commit messages and cover letter.

[PATCH] vfio/container: hold group_lock across backend ioctl passthrough
Author: Jeff Barnes <jeffbarnes@linux.microsoft.com>

vfio_fops_unl_ioctl() forwards unrecognized ioctls to the IOMMU backend
by reading container->iommu_driver and container->iommu_data and calling
driver->ops->ioctl() without holding container->group_lock.

When the last group is detached, vfio_group_detach_container() takes
group_lock for write, releases the backend and clears both pointers.
An ioctl running concurrently on the container fd can therefore use
backend state that is being torn down. With the Type1 backend, a
VFIO_IOMMU_GET_INFO racing with the last group detach walks iommu->
iova_list while vfio_iommu_type1_release() frees it, and faults on a
poisoned list pointer (dead000000000100).

The other container entry points that use the backend, namely
VFIO_CHECK_EXTENSION and VFIO_SET_IOMMU, already take group_lock.
Do the same for the passthrough path and hold it for read across the
backend call. This is consistent with the existing lock order, as
backends only take their own locks (e.g. iommu->lock) below group_lock.

This patch was developed with the assistance of GitHub Copilot CLI
(Claude Sonnet 5.5), which helped analyze the race, draft the fix and
this description, and build-check the result. The author reviewed the
change and tested it on a downstream 6.6 kernel.

Fixes: cba3345cc494 ("vfio: VFIO core")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Jeff Barnes <jeffbarnes@linux.microsoft.com>
---
 drivers/vfio/container.c | 3 +++
 1 file changed, 3 insertions(+)

[PATCH 1/3] KVM: arm64: Add enum support to idreg sanitisation
Author: Marc Zyngier <maz@kernel.org>

While a good deal of ID register fields are simple booleans, simply
indicating whether a feature is supported, a number of them are more
structured and indicate a level of support.

Reuse the existing framework in a nested way to expose which level
applies for the current configuration. This relies on the levels
being ordered from the most to the least capable, and the fallback
to have a NULL callback.

Yes, this is pretty obscure, but the following patches will make
this clearer (at least one hopes...).

[PATCH] KVM: arm64: nv: Upgrade sub-page TTL hint to maximum size
Author: Marc Zyngier <maz@kernel.org>

Should a guest be slightly foolish and submit a S2 TLBI with a TTL hint
that describe a base granule size smaller than the host's page size,
we take the resulting size at face value and feed it to the S2 unmapping
code. Which legitimately screams about something not being page-aligned.

The architecture allows us to drop the TLBI on the floor, as we don't
support a S2 granule size smaller than the host's page size. But it turns
out that doing so is proving pretty hard (we call compute_tlb_inval_range
from a number of places, none of which is amenable to an early out).

Instead, detect the annoying case, and pretend that we don't have a TTL
hint at all, leaving the size upgrade mechanism to take place and use
the largest possible size for the guest's S2 granule size.

This will still allow nonsensical stuff to get through, such as a 4kB
level 2 (aka 2MB) from a guest with a 64kB S2 base granule size running
on a 16kB page size host, but that's not worse than doing nothing, and
the host code won't get shouty.

Reported-by: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261123-inval_range-v1-1-815e15797089@pereq.eu
Fixes: d1de1576dc21 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Handle FEAT_TTL hinted TLB operations")
Signed-off-by: Marc Zyngier <maz@kernel.org>
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
---

Notes:
    Reposting this as a first class patch, as I'd like to plug that one
    as early as possible (and Dan has plenty of other patches in flight...)

[PATCH v10 11/20] KVM: x86: Support EGPR accessing and tracking for emulator
Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Extend the emulator context and GPR accessors to handle EGPRs before
adding support for REX2-prefixed instructions. Like vCPU cache, the
emulator can uniformly cache and track all GPRs.

Also replace `1 << reg` with `BIT(reg)` in reg_read()/reg_write(). The
former performs a signed 32-bit shift, which becomes undefined for R31.
BIT() instead shifts an unsigned value that is suitable for all indices.

[PATCH v10 13/20] KVM: x86: Support REX2-prefixed opcode decode
Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Extend the instruction decoder to recognize and handle the REX2 prefix,
including validation of prefix sequences and correct opcode table
selection. Mark the UD opcode flag explicitly with Undefined, instead
of zeroing.

REX2 is a terminal prefix: once 0xD5 is encountered, the following byte
is the opcode. When REX2.M=0, most prefix bytes are invalid after REX2,
including REX, VEX, EVEX, and another REX2. Also, REX2-prefixed
instructions are only valid in 64-bit mode.

All of the invalid prefix combinations after REX2 coincide with opcodes
that are architecturally invalid in 64-bit mode. Thus, marking such
opcodes with No64 in opcode_table[] naturally disallows those illegal
prefix sequences.

The 0x40 - 0x4F opcode row was missing the No64 flag. While NoRex2
already invalidates REX2 for these opcodes, adding No64 makes opcode
attributes explicit and complete.

Note opcodes marked NotImpl alone end up in handle_emulation_failure(),
but that will inject #UD eventually.

Suggested-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Signed-off-by: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>
Tested-by: Zhao Liu <zhao1.liu@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Jim Mattson <jmattson@google.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/CABgObfYYGTvkYpeyqLSr9JgKMDA_STSff2hXBNchLZuKFU+MMA@mail.gmail.com
---

[PATCH v10 19/20] KVM: selftests: Add APX state and ABI test
Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Test APX-specific behavior and ABI interactions as these are unique
compared to other state components:

    components).

[PATCH v10 16/20] KVM: x86: Guard valid XCR0.APX settings
Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Prevent invalid XCR0.APX configurations in two cases: conflict with MPX
and lack of SVM support.

In the non-compacted XSAVE format, APX and MPX conflict on the same
offset. Although MPX is being deprecated in practice, KVM should
explicitly reject such configurations that set both bits.

At this point, only VMX supports EGPRs. SVM will require corresponding
extensions to handle EGPR indices.

This is in preparation for APX in KVM's supported XCR0.

Suggested-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Prepare the emulator for REX2 handling by introducing the NoRex2 opcode
flag and supporting extended register indices. For the latter, factor out
common logic for calculating register IDs.

REX2 does not support three-byte opcodes. Instead, the REX2.M bit selects
between one- and two-byte opcode tables, which were previously
distinguished by the 0x0F escape byte.

Some legacy instructions in those tables never reference extended
registers. When prefixed with REX, such instructions are treated as if
the prefix were absent. In contrast, a REX2 prefix causes a #UD, which
should be handled explicitly.

JMPABS (0xA1) is an exception: it is defined for REX2 but mark it as not
implemented, since emulating non-memory operation isn't practically
that useful here.

Also, ignore the high-order register index bits when accessing vector
registers (XMM and YMM) or segment registers.

Suggested-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Signed-off-by: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>
Tested-by: Zhao Liu <zhao1.liu@intel.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/1ebf3a23-5671-41c1-8daa-c83f2f105936@redhat.com
---
V9 -> V10: Mask higher bits for segment registers (Jim Mattson)
---

[PATCH v10 04/20] KVM: x86: Support APX state for XSAVE ABI
Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Introduce a facility to copy APX state between the vCPU cache and the
userspace buffer as the vCPU cache is the single source of truth.

The existing fpstate copy functions historically sync all XSTATEs in
between userspace and kernel buffers [1]. In this regard, any additional
state handling logic should be consistent with them -- i.e. validation of
XSTATE_BV against the supported XCR0 mask.

Now the two copy paths must account for these facts:

  * When exporting to userspace, copy fpstate first, since it zeros the
    area of any component that is absent or inactive. Then copy the APX
    state from the vCPU cache.

  * When importing from userspace, the APX state is copied to both
    fpstate and the vCPU cache. Avoiding the duplicate copy would need
    special care in the common code or in XSTATE_BV/XCR0 which isn't
    worth that much for a slow path, so keep it simple.

[1] Except for PKRU state, as stored in struct thread_struct.

Signed-off-by: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>
Tested-by: Zhao Liu <zhao1.liu@intel.com>
---

[PATCH v10 02/20] KVM: VMX: Save guest EGPRs in vCPU cache
Author: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>

Save and restore the guest EGPRs on VM exit/entry if the system supports
Advanced Performance Extensions (APX).

There are two possible ways of handling EGPR accesses: reading them from
the processor lazily, or switching them in the vmentry/vmexit path and
accessing them via vcpu->arch.regs.  Prepare for the latter; it does not
pollute kvm_register_read()/kvm_register_write() with conditionals to
treat GPRs vs. EGPRs differently, and it allows for future usage of APX
registers in the kernel.

KVM intercepts all XCR0 writes, therefore XCR0 cannot change during the
execution of VMLAUNCH/VMRESUME. Thus, for a guest that does not set
XCR0[APX], the EGPR state does not have to be loaded/saved.

The register saving path is placed after SPEC_CTRL is restored, so as
to avoid any mis-speculation into EGPR accesses when APX is disabled.

While saving may be skipped on VM-Fail, it is not worth the added
complication for a slow path.

Suggested-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Chang S. Bae <chang.seok.bae@intel.com>
Tested-by: Zhao Liu <zhao1.liu@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Jim Mattson <jmattson@google.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/adPRA4ZhnvbaXSn0@google.com
---

[PATCH v3 4/5] KVM: x86: nVMX: move nested APICv state to nested_guest_state
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

[PATCH v3 5/5] KVM: x86: nVMX: fix races in delivery of nested posted interrupts
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

If a nested posted interrupt races with a L0 vm exit on a target vCPU,
that is reflected to L1, then this nestedf posted interrupt is currently
neither processed nor delivered to L1 as a regular interrupt.

Since it is not possible to know if APICv hardware has processed
the interrupt, opt to always process it a moment before nested VM exit.
(call vmx_complete_nested_posted_interrupt)

To ensure that vmx_deliver_nested_posted_interrupt and
vmx_complete_nested_posted_interrupt don't race do the following:

Have a cmpxchg based loop in vmx_deliver_nested_posted_interrupt that
atomically checks that target vCPU is in nested mode and has suitable
posted interrupt configuration, and then set KVM_NESTED_PI_PENDING.

If the above cmpxchg loop succeeds, this means that even if the target vCPU
is in process of exiting the nested guest, it will notice this
KVM_NESTED_PI_PENDING flag when it reaches its
vmx_complete_nested_posted_interrupt, and then it will process it.

If it fails, it means that the target vCPU is no longer in the guest
mode, and thus it is safe to deliver the interrupt as a normal interrupt.

Apply a similar logic on the receiving side:
Use xchg to snapshot the old value of guest state in leave_guest_mode,
and by using this snapshot, determine if we need to process
the posted interrupts.

Suggested-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Signed-off-by: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/regs.h       |  5 ++--
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c | 30 ++++++++++++++------
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c    | 60 +++++++++++++++++++--------------------
 3 files changed, 54 insertions(+), 41 deletions(-)

[PATCH v3 1/5] KVM: nVMX: don't check PIR.ON when processing nested posted interrupts
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

Despite the suggestion to test and set the PIR.ON and avoid sending
a posted interrupt if it is already set, there is no requirement
for this bit to be set for posted interrupt processing to happen
when the target CPU receives the posted notification vector.

See section 30.6 POSTED-INTERRUPT PROCESSING:

   "The processor clears the outstanding-notification bit in
    the posted-interrupt descriptor. This is done atomically so as to leave
    the remainder of the descriptor unmodified
    (e.g., with a locked AND operation)."

Apparently Windows' implementation of APICv does exactly this:
it sets bits in PIR, without bothering to also set the PIR.ON.

Signed-off-by: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c | 15 +++++++++++----
 1 file changed, 11 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

[PATCH v3 0/5] KVM: nVMX: fix nested APICv emulation for windows guests
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

Fix two problems that were recently discovered that cause nested APICv=0D
to malfunction on windows with hyperv enabled.=0D
=0D
The first problem is that unlike KVM, windows doesn't set PIR.ON when=0D
sending posted interrupts. X86 spec actually allows this.=0D
=0D
The second problem is that unlilke KVM, which mostly ignores them,=0D
windows depends on accurate delivery of the posted notification=0D
interrupts, and currently it is possible for a posted notification=0D
to get lost it it arrives at the same time as the nested VM exit.=0D
=0D
V2: to avoid spurious notification in L1, process the posted=0D
notification in L2, just before VM exit.=0D
=0D
V3: rework the patch series following the pseudocode that Paolo suggested=0D
(Thanks!!)=0D
=0D
Best regards,=0D
	Maxim Levitsky=0D

[PATCH 1/2] KVM: Disable preemption while pushing to the dirty ring
Author: Siddharth Mishra <smish149@asu.edu>

Disable preemption in kvm_dirty_ring_push() so that another push to the
same dirty ring cannot run in the middle of it.

kvm_dirty_ring_push() selects an entry with dirty_index, fills the entry,
and only then increments dirty_index, all with preemption enabled, e.g.
when vmx_flush_pml_buffer() pushes the logged GPAs on VM-Exit.  If the
vCPU task is preempted in that window, kvm_sched_out() calls
kvm_arch_vcpu_put() while kvm_running_vcpu still points at the vCPU, and
kvm_steal_time_set_preempted() or kvm_xen_runstate_set_preempted() marks
a page dirty, which pushes to the same ring.  That push fills the
selected entry and increments dirty_index, then the interrupted push
overwrites the entry and increments dirty_index again.  The entry after
it is never written, its flags stay zero, and the entries after that one
are dirty.

Userspace harvests the ring in order and stops at the first entry that
is not dirty, so it never harvests any later entry, and the GFN of the
overwritten push is lost.  A VMM that tracks dirty memory with the dirty
ring, e.g. for live migration or to restore a snapshot, then misses every
page that the vCPU dirties afterwards.

Fixes: fb04a1eddb1a ("KVM: X86: Implement ring-based dirty memory tracking")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Siddharth Mishra <smish149@asu.edu>
---
 virt/kvm/dirty_ring.c | 2 ++
 1 file changed, 2 insertions(+)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH 2/2] KVM: selftests: Test dirty ring pushes from vCPU preemption
Author: Siddharth Mishra <smish149@asu.edu>

Add a test that dirties pages in a dirty-logged memslot that also holds
the vCPU's steal-time structure, while a busy thread shares the vCPU
thread's CPU so that the vCPU task is preempted often.  After every
KVM_RUN, harvest the dirty ring and assert that no entry is left dirty,
which fails if kvm_steal_time_set_preempted() pushes to the ring in the
middle of kvm_dirty_ring_push().

Require PML, as flushing the PML buffer on VM-Exit is where KVM pushes
to the dirty ring with preemption enabled.  Pass -S to run without steal
time, and -t to change the run time from the default of 60 seconds.

Signed-off-by: Siddharth Mishra <smish149@asu.edu>

[PATCH 0/2] KVM: Fix lost dirty ring entries when a vCPU is preempted
Author: Siddharth Mishra <smish149@asu.edu>

A push to a vCPU's dirty ring can be interrupted by another push to the
same ring when the vCPU task is preempted, which leaves one entry
unwritten and makes userspace stop harvesting the ring.  Patch 1 fixes
kvm_dirty_ring_push(), patch 2 adds a regression test.

Found while fuzzing. An AI tool helped with the analysis and the patches.

The series is independent of James' series that changes the same
function, and applies with or without it:
https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261117212913.3634487-1-jthoughton@google.com/

Tested on Intel with PML, in a nested VM on mainline at
commit 4843f6873f7c ("Merge tag 'sysctl-7.04-fixes-rc4' of
git://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/sysctl/sysctl") and on the
v7.2.8 host:
- Patch 2 fails within seconds without patch 1 (11 of 12 runs), and
  passes with it (0 failures in 15 runs).
- All x86 and common KVM selftests, with PREEMPT, DEBUG_PREEMPT,
  DEBUG_ATOMIC_SLEEP, PROVE_LOCKING, PROVE_RCU, DEBUG_PAGEALLOC,
  SLUB_DEBUG, DEBUG_OBJECTS_RCU_HEAD, KVM_SMM and KVM_XEN: the same
  results with and without the series.  hardware_disable_test fails and
  xen_shinfo_test triggers a lockdep "Invalid wait context" report in
  kvm_xen_set_evtchn_fast() on both.
- The dirty logging tests with kvm_intel.pml=0: the same results with and
  without the series.
- Builds with W=1: KVM_INTEL=m KVM_AMD=m KVM_WERROR=y, SMP=n,
  allnoconfig, gcc allmodconfig and allyesconfig, clang allmodconfig, and
  clang for arm64, riscv and loongarch.
- KVM-unit-tests (a2891d9) for x86 in the same nested VM: the same
  results with and without the series.  vmx_apicv_test times out and
  vmx reports "Guest didn't run to completion" and a too high RDTSC to
  VM-exit delta on both.
- sparse (84ec065) with C=2 on arch/x86/kvm/: no reports in
  virt/kvm/dirty_ring.c.

Not tested: AMD, the Xen runstate path.

Siddharth Mishra (2):
  KVM: Disable preemption while pushing to the dirty ring
  KVM: selftests: Test dirty ring pushes from vCPU preemption

[PATCH 3/3] KVM: selftests: arm64: Test KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY before first KVM_RUN
Author: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>

Extend nv_pre_fault_memory_test to exercise KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY before
the first KVM_RUN.

Without EL2, cover immediate_exit on the first KVM_RUN, retrying after a
failed first KVM_RUN, and pre-faulting while the vCPU moves from the
main thread to a worker thread for its first run and back to the main
thread for its second run. The two threads are pinned to different CPUs
when at least two are available.

With EL2, additionally cover an EL2 vCPU with ID_AA64MMFR4_EL1.NV_frac
cleared to 0 and HCR_EL2.NV=1 at EL1h (the NULL vncr_tlb reproducer), an
EL2 vCPU with a vEL1 context and asymmetric HCR_EL2.IMO/FMO staged before
the first run, and the same thread handoff. An unfixed kernel reports
the staged context case only as a WARN in the kernel log; the test
itself still passes.

Signed-off-by: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>

 1 file changed, 298 insertions(+), 37 deletions(-)

[PATCH 2/3] KVM: arm64: nv: Don't request or attempt L1 VNCR mapping without vncr_tlb
Author: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>

vcpu->arch.vncr_tlb is only allocated when ID_AA64MMFR4_EL1.NV_frac
advertises FEAT_NV2. When NV_frac is cleared to 0 (or E2H0 is selected),
vncr_tlb is NULL for the lifetime of the vCPU.

Don't set KVM_REQ_MAP_L1_VNCR_EL2 in kvm_vcpu_load_hw_mmu() when
vncr_tlb is NULL. The only other place that sets this request is
kvm_translate_vncr(), which only runs on a FEAT_NV2 VNCR abort. Warn and
return early in kvm_map_l1_vncr() if vt is NULL before disabling
preemption and taking kvm->mmu_lock.

Signed-off-by: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/nested.c | 6 +++++-
 1 file changed, 5 insertions(+), 1 deletion(-)

[PATCH 1/3] KVM: arm64: Don't load hardware vCPU state before first run
Author: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>

KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY calls vcpu_load() as soon as KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT has
completed. On arm64, however, kvm->arch.sysreg_masks, the trap
configuration, vncr_tlb and nested vGIC state are only set up on the
first KVM_RUN in kvm_arch_vcpu_run_pid_change().

Running the full vcpu_load() path before then reads unsanitised guest
sysregs and touches uninitialised nested state: get_s2_mmu_nested()
populates nested_mmus[] from raw VTTBR_EL2/VTCR_EL2/HCR_EL2 values, FGT
and HCRX_EL2 are written before kvm_calculate_traps() has run, and
kvm_vgic_load() can enter vgic_v3_load_nested() early. In particular, if
userspace creates an EL2 vCPU with ID_AA64MMFR4_EL1.NV_frac cleared to 0
(so vncr_tlb is never allocated), sets HCR_EL2.NV=1 and an EL1 PSTATE,
and calls KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY, kvm_vcpu_load_hw_mmu() queues
KVM_REQ_MAP_L1_VNCR_EL2. The next KVM_RUN then dereferences a NULL
vncr_tlb in kvm_map_l1_vncr() with preemption disabled and kvm->mmu_lock
held for read:

  [   73.636014] Unable to handle kernel NULL pointer dereference at virtual address 0000000000000078
  [   73.801972] pc : kvm_map_l1_vncr+0x48/0x2d0
  [   73.884820] Call trace:
  [   73.887253]  kvm_map_l1_vncr+0x48/0x2d0 (P)
  [   73.891422]  check_nested_vcpu_requests+0xb8/0x168
  [   73.896200]  check_vcpu_requests+0x2a0/0x428
  [   73.900458]  kvm_arch_vcpu_ioctl_run+0x2d8/0x6d8
  [   73.905062]  kvm_vcpu_ioctl+0x388/0xaf0

KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY still needs generic vcpu_load() to set
kvm_running_vcpu for dirty-ring tracking in mark_page_dirty_in_slot(),
but kvm_arch_vcpu_pre_fault_memory() only walks and populates
&kvm->arch.mmu and does not use any hardware vCPU state. Skip the arch
vcpu_load()/vcpu_put() callbacks until vcpu_has_run_once() is true. In
KVM_RUN, vcpu->pid is set under vcpu->mutex right after
kvm_arch_vcpu_run_pid_change() succeeds and before
kvm_arch_vcpu_ioctl_run() calls vcpu_load().

Since kvm_running_vcpu can now be set without VHE state on the CPU, use
host_ctxt->__hyp_running_vcpu in VHE enter_vmid_context() (matching
nVHE) and check vcpu_has_run_once() in vgic_maintenance_handler().

Fixes: 34cfa9b3af17 ("KVM: arm64: Implement KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY")
Signed-off-by: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/arm.c            | 17 +++++++++++++++--
 arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/vhe/tlb.c    |  3 ++-
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-init.c |  2 +-
 3 files changed, 18 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

[PATCH 0/3] KVM: arm64: Fix pre-first-run KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY vcpu_load() issues
Author: Jia He <justin.he@arm.com>

KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY calls vcpu_load() as soon as KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT has
completed. On arm64, VM and vCPU state such as kvm->arch.sysreg_masks,
trap controls, vncr_tlb and nested vGIC setup is only initialised on the
first KVM_RUN in kvm_arch_vcpu_run_pid_change().

Running the full arch vcpu_load() path before then reads unsanitised
guest sysregs and touches uninitialised nested state. In particular, if
a VMM creates an EL2 vCPU with ID_AA64MMFR4_EL1.NV_frac cleared to 0,
sets HCR_EL2.NV=1 and an EL1 PSTATE, and calls KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY,
kvm_vcpu_load_hw_mmu() queues KVM_REQ_MAP_L1_VNCR_EL2 without a
vncr_tlb. The next KVM_RUN then dereferences NULL in kvm_map_l1_vncr()
with preemption disabled and kvm->mmu_lock held for read.

Patch 1 skips the arch vcpu_load()/vcpu_put() callbacks until
vcpu_has_run_once() is true, and adjusts VHE enter_vmid_context() and
vgic_maintenance_handler() accordingly. Generic vcpu_load() still sets
kvm_running_vcpu for dirty-ring tracking, and pre-faulting only populates
&kvm->arch.mmu.

Patch 2 checks that vncr_tlb is allocated before setting
KVM_REQ_MAP_L1_VNCR_EL2 and adds a WARN_ON_ONCE(!vt) check in
kvm_map_l1_vncr().

Patch 3 extends nv_pre_fault_memory_test to cover pre-first-run
pre-faulting. The cases that do not need EL2 run on all hosts. On an
unfixed kernel, the staged nested context case triggers the "Separate
virtual IRQ/FIQ settings not supported" WARN while the test still
passes, so the kernel log must be checked as well.

Jia He (3):
  KVM: arm64: Don't load hardware vCPU state before first run
  KVM: arm64: nv: Don't request or attempt L1 VNCR mapping without
    vncr_tlb
  KVM: selftests: arm64: Test KVM_PRE_FAULT_MEMORY before first KVM_RUN

[PATCH] vhost-vdpa: skip unmapped gap in vhost_vdpa_va_map()
Author: Fang Xieyan <fangxy@xiaopeng.com>

find_vma() returns the first VMA with vm_end > uaddr; when uaddr lies
in an unmapped gap, vma->vm_start > uaddr and the offset arithmetic

	offset = (vma->vm_pgoff << PAGE_SHIFT) + uaddr - vma->vm_start;

produces an incorrect file offset, potentially wrapping in u64
arithmetic.

The incorrect offset is stored in map_file->offset and passed to
the mapping backend. Meanwhile, map_size includes the gap, causing
the corresponding IOVA range to be mapped without valid backing.

Skip the gap: stop if the whole remaining request is gap, otherwise
shrink size, pin uaddr to vm_start and advance map_iova by the gap.
The gap is left unmapped without error.

Testing:
- make W=1 drivers/vhost/vdpa.o
- QEMU/KVM v7.4-rc1 with a probe vDPA parent; tested partial
  gap, whole-gap and contiguous IOTLB updates.

Fixes: d8945ec41120 ("vdpa: Support transferring virtual addressing during DMA mapping")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Fang Xieyan <fangxy@xiaopeng.com>
---
 drivers/vhost/vdpa.c | 14 ++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 14 insertions(+)

[PATCH 3/3] vdpa/mlx5: Don't flush the workqueue under reslock
Author: Dragos Tatulea <dtatulea@nvidia.com>

The control VQ work (mlx5_cvq_kick_handler()) runs on mvdev->wq and
takes reslock. Device reset and the error path of .set_status take
reslock and then call unregister_link_notifier() which flushes the same
workqueue. A deadlock occurs if a CVQ kick arrives just before the reset
for the CVQ work.

Lockdep caught this when QEMU quits (pkill qemu) and closes the
vhost-vdpa device:

 ======================================================
 WARNING: possible circular locking dependency detected
 ------------------------------------------------------
 qemu-system-x86/5785 is trying to acquire lock:
 ff110001cd292940 ((wq_completion)mlx5_vdpa_wq){+.+.}-{0:0}, at: touch_wq_lockdep_map+0x7a/0x180

 but task is already holding lock:
 ff11000136eb4928 (&ndev->reslock){+.+.}-{4:4}, at: mlx5_vdpa_compat_reset+0xed/0x760 [mlx5_vdpa]

 which lock already depends on the new lock.

 the existing dependency chain (in reverse order) is:

 -> #2 (&ndev->reslock){+.+.}-{4:4}:
        lock_acquire+0x126/0x330
        down_write+0x87/0x1d0
        mlx5_cvq_kick_handler+0xb1/0x1180 [mlx5_vdpa]
        process_one_work+0x8df/0x1660
        worker_thread+0x62c/0x1030
        kthread+0x377/0x490
        ret_from_fork+0x4d7/0x720
        ret_from_fork_asm+0x11/0x20

 -> #1 ((work_completion)(&ndev->cvq_ent.work)){+.+.}-{0:0}:
        lock_acquire+0x126/0x330
        process_one_work+0x874/0x1660
        worker_thread+0x62c/0x1030
        kthread+0x377/0x490
        ret_from_fork+0x4d7/0x720
        ret_from_fork_asm+0x11/0x20

 -> #0 ((wq_completion)mlx5_vdpa_wq){+.+.}-{0:0}:
        check_prev_add+0xe8/0xdb0
        __lock_acquire+0x1801/0x23e0
        lock_acquire+0x126/0x330
        touch_wq_lockdep_map+0x93/0x180
        __flush_workqueue+0xed/0x1090
        unregister_link_notifier+0x10c/0x170 [mlx5_vdpa]
        mlx5_vdpa_compat_reset+0xfe/0x760 [mlx5_vdpa]
        mlx5_vdpa_reset+0x15/0x30 [mlx5_vdpa]
        _compat_vdpa_reset+0x29b/0x3b0 [vhost_vdpa]
        vhost_vdpa_release+0x16f/0x380 [vhost_vdpa]
        __fput+0x36a/0xaa0
        task_work_run+0x111/0x1f0
        do_exit+0x7ba/0x2590
        do_group_exit+0xb8/0x250
        get_signal+0x1f46/0x2020
        arch_do_signal_or_restart+0x79/0x4a0
        exit_to_user_mode_loop+0xbe/0x450
        do_syscall_64+0x257/0x4f0
        entry_SYSCALL_64_after_hwframe+0x4b/0x53

 other info that might help us debug this:

 Chain exists of:
   (wq_completion)mlx5_vdpa_wq --> (work_completion)(&ndev->cvq_ent.work) --> &ndev->reslock

  Possible unsafe locking scenario:

        CPU0                    CPU1
        ----                    ----
   lock(&ndev->reslock);
                                lock((work_completion)(&ndev->cvq_ent.work));
                                lock(&ndev->reslock);
   lock((wq_completion)mlx5_vdpa_wq);

  *** DEADLOCK ***

 locks held by qemu-system-x86/5785: 3, last CPU#9:
  #0: ff1100021d7c2068 (&dev->mutex#3){+.+.}-{4:4}, at: vhost_vdpa_release+0x4b/0x380 [vhost_vdpa]
  #1: ff11000136eb44c8 (&vdev->cf_lock){++++}-{4:4}, at: _compat_vdpa_reset+0x16b/0x3b0 [vhost_vdpa]
  #2: ff11000136eb4928 (&ndev->reslock){+.+.}-{4:4}, at: mlx5_vdpa_compat_reset+0xed/0x760 [mlx5_vdpa]

vhost_vdpa_release() resets the device before stopping the kick
handlers, so CVQ kicks can still be queued while the reset runs.

The fix is to move unregister_link_notifier() calls outside of
reslock. The function doesn't need the reslock.

Fixes: c384c2401eed ("vdpa/mlx5: Avoid losing link state updates")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dragos Tatulea <dtatulea@nvidia.com>

 1 file changed, 7 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

[PATCH 1/3] vdpa/mlx5: Fix error cleanup when VQ setup fails in .dev_add
Author: Dragos Tatulea <dtatulea@nvidia.com>

When setup_vq_resources() fails in mlx5_vdpa_dev_add(), e.g. because the
firmware rejects the provisioned features (HOST_TSO4/6 without CSUM),
the error path unregisters the vdpa device first and then keeps using
it:

- _vdpa_unregister_device() drops the last reference to the device, so
  mlx5_vdpa_free() runs and ndev is freed.
- destroy_workqueue(mvdev->wq) then reads the freed ndev.
- put_device() on the freed device underflows the refcount:

  refcount_t: underflow; use-after-free.
  WARNING: lib/refcount.c:28 at refcount_warn_saturate+0x59/0x90
  Call Trace:
   mlx5_vdpa_dev_add+0x3e0/0x760 [mlx5_vdpa]
   vdpa_nl_cmd_dev_add_set_doit+0x362/0x440 [vdpa]
   ...

- mgtdev->ndev is left pointing to the freed ndev, so every following
  "vdpa dev add" on this management device fails with -ENOSPC.

mlx5_vdpa_dev_del() already does the cleanup properly so use that. Just
make sure that an extra reference is taken during the call to avoid
_vdpa_unregister_device() from freeing the device under us.

Fixes: ffb1aae43ed5 ("vdpa/mlx5: Pre-create hardware VQs at vdpa .dev_add time")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dragos Tatulea <dtatulea@nvidia.com>

[PATCH 2/3] vdpa/mlx5: Increase the maximum virtqueue size
Author: Dragos Tatulea <dtatulea@nvidia.com>

mlx5_vdpa has always advertised a maximum virtqueue size of 256 through
.get_vq_num_max(), while accepting any queue size in .set_vq_num(). The
hardware handles larger rings just fine and users have been running with
1024-entry queues (e.g. QEMU's virtio-net rx_queue_size/tx_queue_size)
since the driver was introduced.

The commit in the fixes tag vhost-vdpa enforces the advertised maximum
on VHOST_SET_VRING_NUM, so configuring a 1024-entry queue fails with
-EINVAL.

Advertise 1024 which is the max that qemu uses today. The device itself
supports more but doesn't advertise it in any way.

The Fixes tag points at the vhost-vdpa commit rather than at the driver
because that is where the mismatch became user visible. It also keeps
this change out of trees without the vhost enforcement, where virtio-vdpa
would otherwise start creating 1024-entry rings instead of 256.

Fixes: ccb1dc7c527f ("vhost/vdpa: reject VRING_NUM larger than device max")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dragos Tatulea <dtatulea@nvidia.com>
---
 drivers/vdpa/mlx5/net/mlx5_vnet.c | 2 +-
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+), 1 deletion(-)

[PATCH 6.18.y 4/4] KVM: x86: Set guest's fixed-1 DR6 bits when delivering #DB payload
Author: Baoli Zhang <baoli.zhang@linux.intel.com>


commit c62ffee78db8837a6251af83798907af37b53ed4 upstream.

When delivering a #DB payload, set all DR6 bits that are fixed-1 from the
guest's perspective, not just the host's perspective.  Don't WARN on a
"bad" payload (or existing DR6) even though KVM itself shouldn't set DR6
or synthesize a payload without the fixed-1 bits set, as userspace can
provide a completely arbitrary payload via KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS (and
"hardening" that flow is not worth the effort as there's no benefit in
practice).

Return an "unsigned long" instead of a "u64" from the helper to get the
fixed-1 DR6 bits, as debug registers are natural width registers, not
